## management-governance — module synthesis

_Source: `99_management-governance-synthesis.md`_

# Management-Governance Module — V (Synthesis)

## Abstract

Visa is professionally run: no controller is identified, the board has an independent chair and 10 of 11 directors are independent, while the six current executive officers have median Visa tenure of 9.7 years. Capital allocation has increased per-share value—$47.1bn of FY23–FY25 buybacks cut the as-converted share count 2.7% in FY25—but 9M FY26 cash uses exceeded operating cash flow and strict net debt rose. Pay is 72–77% equity for the CEO and CFO, yet bonus weights are undisclosed and insiders own only 0.125%, with no open-market buys. The biggest risk is disclosed legal pressure: active DOJ and EC matters plus recurring litigation add-backs. Verdict: standard, mixed stewardship; capable operators, incomplete alignment and a coverage-limited legal record.

## 1. Stewardship Verdict

- **Verdict: Standard / mixed.**
- **NON-NEGOTIABLE GATE: PASS** — 122 of 179 checklist items are answered Green, Amber, or Red. `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` reports no hard disqualifier; no Critical module flag fired; no controller, CEO, CFO, or board chair is graded Disqualifying; and the legal reconciliation found no material public matter absent from Visa's filings, subject to incomplete PACER, state, and foreign-docket coverage [Business-Model Disqualifier Scan — V, §3; People & Network Integrity Dossiers — V, §1; Regulatory, Legal & Compliance — V, §§1, 8].
- **Hard disqualifier flagged (business-model/01)? N.** Reported verbatim: **“No disqualifier triggered”** and **“Any disqualifier triggered: N.”** No governance-risk floor or verdict lock flows from the scan [Business-Model Disqualifier Scan — V, §§2–3].
- Management quality /100: **78** [Management & Track Record — V, Management Quality Score].
- Capital allocation /100: **73** [Capital-Allocation Scorecard — V, Capital Allocation Score].
- Incentive alignment /100: **78** [Incentives & Compensation — V, Incentive Alignment Score].
- Shareholder friendliness /100: **63** — equal blend of Ownership Alignment **47** and Board & Shareholder Rights **79**, because the former measures economic skin in the game and the latter measures board and minority-rights structure; neither is discarded [Ownership & Insider Behavior — V, Ownership Alignment Score; Board & Shareholder Rights — V, Board & Shareholder Rights Score].
- Disclosure candor /100: **59** [Candor & Disclosure Quality — V, Disclosure Candor Score].
- People & network integrity /100: **65** — capped from the specialist's evidence score of 77 by the coverage-limited legal/database pass and the declared `budget_exhausted` scope boundary [People & Network Integrity Dossiers — V, People & Network Integrity Score].
- Audit & assurance quality /100: **63** [Audit & Assurance Quality — V, Audit & Assurance Quality Score].
- Related-party transaction (RPT) & leakage risk /100 *(INVERTED — higher = worse)*: **42** [Related-Party & Group Forensics — V, RPT & Leakage Risk Score].
- Contingent-liability risk /100 *(INVERTED — higher = worse)*: **43** [Contingent Liabilities & Commitments — V, Contingent-Liability Risk Score].
- Accounting-forensics risk /100 *(INVERTED — higher = worse)*: **15** [Accounting Forensics — V, Accounting-Forensics Risk Score].
- Legal & regulatory risk /100 *(INVERTED — higher = worse)*: **52** [Regulatory, Legal & Compliance — V, Bottom line and §9].
- Governance risk /100 *(INVERTED — higher = worse)*: **45** — one board-entrenchment Red, recurring adjusted-earnings exclusions, audit-firm discipline, and live disclosed enforcement are balanced against no controller, no reported pledge, no identified restatement, no proven leakage, and no undisclosed material case.
- Data quality /100: **82** — 23 source files, 44 workbook tabs, zero extraction failures, and full annual, interim, proxy, compensation, ownership, fee, and transcript coverage; legal databases, rating history, RPT amounts, voting results, and some person/entity registers remain incomplete [Governance Data Triage — V, §§1–5].
- Overall usefulness /100: **78** — useful for the master synthesis, but the legal/person clearance is not complete.
- **Checklist coverage: 122/179 (68.2%)** — **55 Green · 62 Amber · 5 Red · 57 N-A**, where N-A comprises 17 Not Applicable rows (including one “no data” row) and 40 Insufficient Data rows.
- Insider ownership: directors and executive officers beneficially own **0.125%** of Class A shares on the proxy basis, only **0.043%** directly; the trailing-12-month open-market record contains **0 buys, 4 sells, net 32,180 shares sold** [FY26 DEF 14A, Beneficial Ownership, pp.97, 110–111; Capital IQ Insider Trading, latest filed 2026-07-31 — vendor export].
- Biggest governance signal: Visa returned **$59.732bn** through buybacks and dividends in FY23–FY25—$47.130bn plus $12.602bn—while the as-converted share count fell 2.7% in FY25, but 9M FY26 total cash uses of **$26.552bn** exceeded cash from operations (CFO) of **$16.342bn** by $10.210bn and strict net debt—debt minus cash equivalents—rose $3.492bn to $11.499bn [Capital-Allocation Scorecard — V, §§1–4; Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Consolidated Cash Flows and Balance Sheet].
- **Checklist Risk = max(RPT 42, contingent liability 43, accounting forensics 15, legal/regulatory 52) = 52.** Legal and regulatory risk binds; the four risks are not averaged.
- **Governance Score /100 = 65** (unrounded **64.84**): `0.14×73 + 0.11×78 + 0.11×63 + 0.10×59 + 0.11×78 + 0.09×63 + 0.11×65 + 0.11×(100−45) + 0.12×(100−52)` = `10.22 + 8.58 + 6.93 + 5.90 + 8.58 + 5.67 + 7.15 + 6.05 + 5.76 = 64.84`.
- **Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score /100 = 45** (64.84 × 70 ÷ 100 = 45.39).
- **Governance Rating: Watchlist** (55–69 band).
- **Confidence Score /100: 70** — at the binding ceiling for the coverage-limited legal/database sweep; the `budget_exhausted` network cap would otherwise permit 75.
- **Red-Flag Count: 4 / Critical Red-Flag Count: 0.** The four unique triggered IDs are RF-DISC-002, RF-AUD-007, RF-FIN-003, and RF-REG-001; repeated rows under the same ID are not double-counted.

## 1A. Module Disconfirmation

- **Strongest bear point:** legal and regulatory exposure is live and core to the business. The DOJ debit-network action survived Visa's dismissal motion, the EC fee inquiry remains open, and recognised litigation plus estimated gross unrecognised tax benefits total **$3.005bn**, or **8.5%** of June 2026 equity; possible-loss tails remain unquantified [DOJ, *U.S. v. Visa Inc.*, case page updated 2025-07-07; FY25 Form 10-K, Note 20, pp.99–107; Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Notes 15–16, pp.23–28].
- **Strongest bull point:** the primary accounting record does not show manipulation or financial fragility. FY25 CFO/net income was **1.15×**, CFO/GAAP-derived EBITDA—earnings before interest, tax, depreciation and amortisation—was **91.5%**, and the Beneish M-score, a screen for patterns common in manipulated accounts, was **−2.461** with zero banded components in the manipulator zone; strict net debt/EBITDA was **0.32×** [FY25 Form 10-K, pp.59–65; Accounting Forensics — V, §§2, 6, 11].
- **Single killer risk specific to stewardship:** management continues large distributions while an adverse antitrust remedy changes Visa's core debit conduct or economics and the unestimated legal tail crystallises. The risk is not the booked $1.274bn litigation accrual alone; it is an injunction or settlement that changes future economics while 9M FY26 cash uses already exceeded internally generated cash [Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Note 16, pp.24–28; Capital-Allocation Scorecard — V, §1].
- **Disconfirming evidence already visible:** Visa disclosed the DOJ, EC, U.S. and European interchange matters; the external reconciliation found zero material public matters absent from the filings; legal provisions and payments are bridged, including **$3.142bn** paid in 9M FY26; and neither the disqualifier scan nor the people register found proven fraud, debarment, sanctions, or a Disqualifying person [Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Note 16, pp.24–28; Regulatory, Legal & Compliance — V, §8; People & Network Integrity Dossiers — V, §1]. This weakens a concealment thesis but does not remove the economic risk or incomplete sweep.

## 3. Reconciliation

1. **Ownership 47 versus board and rights 79.** These measure different things. Visa's structure is comparatively shareholder-friendly—independent chair, 10 of 11 independent directors, annual majority elections—but directors and executives have little personal capital at risk and made no open-market purchases. The formula requires one Shareholder-friendliness input, so the two equally relevant specialist scores are averaged: `(47 + 79) ÷ 2 = 63`; the stronger board structure does not erase weak skin in the game.
2. **Candor 59 versus legal-provision disclosure.** Agent 10 finds transparent provision/payment bridges and agent 12 reconciles all identified material public matters to the filings. Agent 06 still correctly fires RF-DISC-002 because the same litigation costs are repeatedly excluded from adjusted performance. Reconciliation: Visa is specific about the numbers but aggressive about which recurring costs it asks investors to ignore; no claim of GAAP misstatement is made.
3. **Accounting risk 15 versus recurring add-backs.** FY25 CFO/PAT of 1.15×, CFO/EBITDA of 91.5% and M-score −2.461 point away from manipulated accounts. They do not answer whether non-GAAP presentation is conservative. The accounting books read cleaner than the adjusted narrative; both findings stand.
4. **People register versus entity legal risk.** No person is graded Material or Disqualifying, while Visa itself faces DOJ and EC matters. The government actions are against the issuer and do not establish personal misconduct by the CEO, CFO or chair. Person grades remain qualified by civil-litigation status and incomplete identifiers.
5. **Reported-zero RPT ratios versus RPT risk 42.** The business-model disqualifier scan calculates 0% from no reported Item 404 amount, solely to test the >25% lock, and explicitly says this is not proof that activity is economically zero. Agent 09's missing-numerator treatment is the correct ongoing risk read; the lock stays N, but the RPT score does not fall to zero.
6. **Gross settlement exposure versus recognised contingencies.** The $168.6bn maximum daily settlement exposure is a peak gross flow, not a point-in-time liability and cannot be added to $3.005bn of recognised litigation plus estimated gross tax uncertainty. The latter is 8.5% of June equity and drives RF-FIN-003; the former is monitored as scale/liquidity exposure on an explicitly unmatched basis.
7. **Dedicated capital allocation 73 versus business-model quick-read 80.** `business-model/11` mixed audit, litigation, working-capital and capital-allocation severities and did not carry the dedicated module's complete $67.582bn FY23–FY25 uses build or the 9M FY26 funding gap. This module uses agent 02's 73, while preserving the quick-read's correct conclusion that Visa is not a serial acquirer. This synthesis supersedes that quick-read.

## 5. Stewardship Summary

Visa's managers have delivered growth and per-share value in the observed record: earnings per share (EPS) rose 23.2% from FY23 to FY25, the share count fell, and the two resolved FY26 guidance comparisons were met or exceeded. Capital returns are real, but 9M FY26 uses exceeded operating cash flow and buyback intrinsic value is not assessable, so the record does not prove that every repurchased dollar created value. Pay is heavily equity-based and includes EPS and relative total shareholder return, but the annual bonus also rewards scale measures with undisclosed weights; low insider ownership and no open-market buying weaken the personal-capital alignment. Minority holders benefit from a mostly independent board and annual majority elections, but long tenures, no written consent, a 15% special-meeting threshold, and limited vote-result history stop the rights read from being exemplary. The reason to trust this team is the combination of delivered operating results, transparent filed legal bridges, and clean measured accounting; the reason not to grant high conviction is the disclosed antitrust overhang, recurring adjusted add-backs, and incomplete legal/person sweep.

## 6. What Would Change The Stewardship Verdict?

| Current Verdict | What Would Strengthen It | What Would Weaken It | Data Needed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Standard / mixed | DOJ/EC matters resolve without material economic remedy; FY26 shows cash uses back within CFO; buybacks keep reducing diluted shares at demonstrably attractive prices; bonus weights become transparent; clean bad-quarter Q&A; complete person/entity sweep stays clear | Adverse antitrust injunction or large unprovided loss; another year of distributions materially above CFO funded by debt; recurring adjusted exclusions widen; a proven integrity finding or undisclosed material case; partner/component audit weakness; RPT amounts reveal leakage | **Highest-value next request: a complete, current official legal-regulatory dossier covering the DOJ docket, direct EC case file/status, PACER/state/foreign dockets, sanctions, rating actions, and the four predecessor entities.** This single package would test the binding 52 LegalRisk and lift or confirm the 70 confidence ceiling |

## 7. Note To The Final Synthesizer

- **Primary verdict:** Standard / mixed stewardship; Governance Score **65**, Watchlist rating, Confidence **70**. The strongest evidence is real per-share delivery—$47.130bn of FY23–FY25 buybacks and a 2.7% FY25 as-converted share-count decline—tempered by 9M FY26 cash uses at 162.5% of CFO.
- **Gate:** PASS, with **122/179** checklist items answered. No hard disqualifier, no Critical flag, no Disqualifying controller/CEO/CFO/chair, and no material public matter proved absent from filings.
- **Capital allocation:** score **73**. Dividends are well covered and share count fell; acquisition returns and buyback intrinsic value are not proven, and strict net debt rose in 9M FY26.
- **Alignment:** equity-heavy pay and EPS/relative-TSR measures help, but undisclosed bonus weights, size/activity metrics, 0.125% insider ownership, zero open-market buys and four sells keep alignment mixed.
- **People:** 11 Clean, 13 Minor, 0 Material, 0 Disqualifying; riskiest are McInerney, Suh and Andreski because of Cai, dismissed with leave to amend and no merits ruling. No open §24 Filter 1 signal.
- **Checklist:** 68.2% coverage; Red items are A1-04, A4-02, A4-13, A9-01 and A9-02. Legal & regulatory risk **52** is the binding Checklist Risk.
- **Biggest flag:** RF-REG-001 (High), from disclosed DOJ and EC matters. RF-DISC-002, RF-AUD-007 and RF-FIN-003 also remain High; none is Critical.
- **Disqualifier:** verbatim upstream result: **“No disqualifier triggered”** and **“Any disqualifier triggered: N.”**
- **§24 rejector filters:** turnaround, serial acquirer, unaligned owner and unresolved integrity filters did **not** trip. No rejector-filter score cap applies.
- **Partial-data caps:** coverage-limited legal/database work caps People & Network Integrity at 65 and Confidence at 70; overall query-budget exhaustion independently caps PNI at 65 and Confidence at 75. The legal ceiling binds.
- **Biggest missing data:** the complete official legal-regulatory dossier specified in Section 6.
- **Handoff:** this module **supersedes `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`**. Use this report's governance verdict and scores as the primary governance read; retain the quick-read only as background.

## 8. Simple Summary

- Management has delivered in the observed window, but the guidance record contains only two resolved quarterly tests and no genuine miss.
- Buybacks and dividends created visible per-share results; 9M FY26 cash deployment ran ahead of operating cash flow.
- Pay is mostly equity and uses EPS and relative shareholder return, but annual-bonus weights are hidden and some goals reward size.
- Insiders own only 0.125%; there were no open-market buys and four sells in the trailing 12 months.
- Minority holders get an independent chair and majority voting, but long director tenures, no written consent and limited vote history weaken the structure.
- Disclosure is specific, yet recurring litigation and acquisition costs are removed from adjusted results; candor in a bad quarter is untested.
- No hard disqualifier or Critical red flag fired; the DOJ/EC legal overhang is the largest High-severity risk.
- This module is useful for the master synthesizer, with confidence capped at 70 until the legal/person sweep is completed.

## 6. Sufficiency Verdict

- **Verdict:** Sufficient
- **Reason:** The frozen pool contains an intact FY25 DEF 14A with compensation, ownership, board and related-person disclosure; CIQ ownership and insider-transaction exports; audited FY25 and quarterly filing history; and three current FY26 transcripts.
- **Specialists that can run:** management track record, capital allocation, incentives, ownership, board, candor, people dossiers (filing-seeded discovery), audit quality, RPT/group forensics, contingent liabilities, accounting forensics, regulatory/legal.
- **Hard disqualifier already flagged by business-model/01_disqualifier-scan?** N — upstream scan says no disqualifier triggered.
- **Active partial-data caps:** None at triage. Agent 09 must explicitly determine whether transaction-level RPT amounts are disclosed; agents 07 and 12 must state their own database-sweep coverage rather than infer it from this inventory.
- **Critical missing items:** FY26 voting-result filing / shareholder vote outcome; rating-agency rationale; a standalone shareholder letter. These do not prevent the required governance specialists from running.
- **Single highest-value missing document:** FY26 annual-meeting voting results / Form 8-K, because it is the direct evidence on shareholder dissent and is absent from the frozen pool.

# Management & Track Record — V

## 1. Key Executives

| Role | Name | Appointed | Tenure | Relevant Background | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| CEO | Ryan McInerney | President May 2013; CEO February 2023 | 13.4 years at Visa; 3.6 years as CEO | Previously CEO of JPMorgan Chase Consumer Banking; before that a principal in McKinsey’s retail-banking and payments practices. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Director Nominee Biographies—Ryan McInerney”] |
| CFO | Chris Suh | Joined Visa as CFO July 2023 | 3.2 years | Former CFO of Electronic Arts (March 2022–June 2023); previously finance leadership roles at Microsoft, including CFO of Cloud + AI. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Executive Officers,” pp.47–48] |
| President, Technology | Rajat Taneja | Joined Visa November 2013; current-title appointment date not disclosed | 12.9 years | Former Electronic Arts CTO; previously Microsoft Commerce and Online Services executive. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Executive Officers,” pp.47–48] |
| Chief Risk and Client Services Officer | Paul D. Fabara | Joined Visa September 2019; current-title appointment date not disclosed | 7.0 years | Former American Express Global Services president and Global Risk, Banking & Compliance leader; formerly Barclaycard COO. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Executive Officers,” pp.47–48] |
| General Counsel | Julie B. Rottenberg | Joined Visa February 2008; current-title appointment date not disclosed | 18.6 years | Former Visa deputy and North America chief counsel; formerly an Arnold & Porter partner. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Executive Officers,” pp.47–48] |
| Vice Chair, Chief People and Corporate Affairs Officer, and Corporate Secretary | Kelly Mahon Tullier | Joined Visa June 2014; current-title appointment date not disclosed | 12.3 years | Former Visa general counsel and former PepsiCo senior legal executive. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Executive Officers,” pp.47–48] |

McInerney is a professional manager, not a founder or owner-operator: the proxy presents his external career and Visa appointment history, and does not identify an executive founder or controlling executive shareholder. [FY26 DEF 14A, “Director Nominee Biographies—Ryan McInerney”; Beneficial Ownership tables, pp.110–111]

## 2. Management Stability

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Senior departures (last 3 years) | Not fully assessable. The frozen pool has a current officer roster but no three-year set of 8-K management-change disclosures or prior officer rosters. The six disclosed current executive officers have a median Visa tenure of 9.7 years. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Executive Officers,” pp.47–48; “Director Nominee Biographies—Ryan McInerney”] |
| CFO turnover | One CFO appointment is visible: Suh joined in July 2023. The available evidence does not establish serial CFO turnover or the prior CFO’s departure circumstances. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Executive Officers,” pp.47–48] |
| CEO succession / continuity | McInerney moved from a near-decade as President (May 2013–January 2023) to CEO in February 2023. This is internal continuity, not evidence of a new rescue plan. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Director Nominee Biographies—Ryan McInerney”] |

The one identified CFO change is not, by itself, a turnover warning. But the missing three-year officer-change archive means that a clean churn record is not proven from the available data.

## 3. Stated Strategy & Targets

Management’s stated growth plan has four lanes: consumer, commercial and money-movement payments; AI and agentic commerce; stablecoins and blockchain; and value-added services. [Visa Q2 FY26 Earnings Call, CEO prepared remarks, 2026-04-28] The explicit current targets are near-term rather than long-term: Q4 FY26 GAAP net-revenue growth of low-double-digit to low-teens and FY26 growth at the low end of mid-teens; on an adjusted constant-dollar basis, the respective targets are high-end low-double-digit and low-end low-teens. [Visa Q3 FY26 Financial Results Presentation, pp.20–21]

No multi-year numeric margin, return-on-capital, leverage, or synergy target was identified in the supplied management materials. That is a disclosure limit, not evidence that management has no internal targets.

## 4. Promise vs Delivery

| Prior Promise / Target | Period Set | Actual Outcome | Kept / Missed | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Adjusted constant-dollar net-revenue growth in the low double digits; adjusted EPS growth at the high end of low double digits. | Q1 FY26 call, for Q2 FY26 | Q2 adjusted constant-dollar net-revenue growth was 15%; adjusted constant-dollar EPS growth was 20%. | Kept / exceeded. The guidance uses qualitative bands, so the conclusion is directional rather than a precise point-estimate beat. | [Visa Q1 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-01-29, pp.9–10; Visa Q2 FY26 Financial Results Presentation, p.1] |
| Adjusted net-revenue growth in the low double digits; adjusted EPS growth in the mid- to high-single digits. | Q2 FY26 call, for Q3 FY26 | Q3 adjusted constant-dollar net-revenue growth was 12%; adjusted constant-dollar EPS growth was 10%. | Kept / exceeded. Revenue landed in the stated qualitative band and EPS was above the stated high-single-digit range. | [Visa Q2 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-04-28; Visa Q3 FY26 Financial Results Presentation, p.1] |
| FY26 adjusted net-revenue growth in the low double digits and adjusted EPS growth in the low double digits. | Q1 FY26 call, full FY26 | Only Q1–Q3 FY26 have been reported as of 2026-09-23; the Q4 outcome is unavailable. | Not assessable yet. | [Visa Q1 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-01-29, pp.9–10; Visa Q3 FY26 Financial Results Presentation, pp.20–21] |
| Supplementary execution check, not management guidance: revenue and normalized EPS versus vendor consensus. | FQ4 FY25–FQ3 FY26 | Revenue and normalized EPS beat consensus in each of four reported quarters. GAAP EPS beat only once in four because the conclusion changes with special items and the vendor’s definition differs from Visa’s non-GAAP EPS. | Supportive but not a substitute for guidance accuracy. | [earnings/04_guidance-consensus, 2026-09-23, §6; Capital IQ Estimates → Surprise, FQ4 FY25–FQ3 FY26 — vendor export] |

The two resolved management-guidance tests were met, but they cover only Q2 and Q3 FY26. They do not establish a full-cycle record or validate the still-open FY26 guide.

| If yes: years of *proven* operating inflection actually delivered (not promised) | Not applicable: the available read is not a turnaround thesis. | [FY26 DEF 14A, “Director Nominee Biographies—Ryan McInerney”] |
| Résumé / plan / consultant deck cited as the reason to believe? (not evidence) | No. The positive part of this read rests on two resolved quarterly guidance comparisons; résumés and strategy statements are context only. | [Visa Q1 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-01-29, pp.9–10; Visa Q2 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-04-28; Visa Q2 and Q3 FY26 Financial Results Presentations, p.1] |

This is not an independent people-integrity sweep. | [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan, 2026-09-23, §§2–4] |

## 5. Management Read

Visa has a stable disclosed senior team: the six current executive officers’ median Visa tenure is 9.7 years, while the CEO has been inside the company since 2013 and CEO since February 2023. [FY26 DEF 14A, “Executive Officers,” pp.47–48; “Director Nominee Biographies—Ryan McInerney”] The two fully observable FY26 quarterly guides were met or exceeded on their own adjusted, constant-dollar bases. [Visa Q1 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-01-29, pp.9–10; Visa Q2 and Q3 FY26 Financial Results Presentations, p.1]

The limiting signal is sample length, not a documented miss: the pool does not establish three-year management turnover, a full-cycle promise record, or the still-open FY26 outcome. This supports a solid but not exceptional management-quality score; it is not a claim of a long-term record across different economic conditions.

## management-governance / 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md

_Source: `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`_

# Capital-Allocation Scorecard — V

Visa Inc. (NYSE: V) reports in USD under U.S. GAAP and has a September fiscal year-end. Unless stated otherwise, the three-year scorecard period is FY23–FY25 (years ended September 30); the Q3 FY26 update is kept separate because it is a nine-month, unaudited period.

## 1. Uses of Capital (3–5 years)

| Use | Cumulative Amount | Currency | % of CFO (or FCF) | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---|
| Acquisitions (net cash acquired) | 1,802 | USD m | 2.8% of $63,764m CFO | [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65] |
| Buybacks (cash-flow basis) | 47,130 | USD m | 73.9% of CFO | [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65] |
| Dividends | 12,602 | USD m | 19.8% of CFO | [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65] |
| Capex (property, equipment and technology) | 3,798 | USD m | 6.0% of CFO | [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65] |
| Debt repayment | 2,250 | USD m | 3.5% of CFO | [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65] |
| **Total identified uses** | **67,582** | **USD m** | **106.0% of CFO** | **Build: 1,802 + 47,130 + 12,602 + 3,798 + 2,250; CFO = 20,755 + 19,950 + 23,059** |

The reported currency is USD. FY23–FY25 CFO was $63.764bn and FCF (cash from operations less total capex) was $59.966bn. The identified uses exceeded CFO by $3.818bn; this was not presented as one funding bridge, but FY25 included $3.924bn of senior-note issuance, so the excess is not evidence that dividends alone were debt-funded. [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65]

**Current-period funding check — Q3 FY26 nine months, not included above.** Cash buybacks ($16.430bn), dividends ($3.852bn), net acquisition cash ($0.705bn), and senior-note repayments ($5.565bn) total $26.552bn, or 162.5% of $16.342bn CFO. The company issued $2.995bn of senior notes in the same period. Strict net debt rose from $8.007bn at September 30, 2025 ($25.171bn debt less $17.164bn cash) to $11.499bn at June 30, 2026 ($23.858bn debt less $12.359bn cash). The CIQ sidecar's $10.066bn is a **broad** net-debt basis: it also nets $1.433bn investment securities, and therefore reconciles to, rather than replaces, the strict figure. [Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows and Balance Sheets, pp.4, 10; `ciq_facts.json`, Jun. 30 2026, `net_debt_m` — CIQ vendor basis]

## 2. M&A Scorecard

| Deal | Year | Price | Disclosed Rationale / Synergy | Delivered? | Opportunity Cost (divestitures forced / focus lost / options foregone) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|---|
| Pismo Holdings | FY24 | $929m purchase consideration; $915m net cash in the cash-flow statement | Cloud-native issuer processing and core banking across Latin America, Asia Pacific and Europe | Pismo is described as part of Visa's issuer-processing/core-banking offering, but no deal-level revenue, synergy, ROIC, or payback outcome is disclosed. **Not proven from available data.** | Net cash was 4.9% of FY24 FCF ($915m / $18.693bn). No deal-specific divestiture, funding linkage, or opportunity-cost measure is disclosed. | [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 2—Acquisitions, p.68; Item 1—Business; Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65] |
| Featurespace | FY25 | $946m purchase consideration; $887m net cash in the cash-flow statement | Real-time behavioural analytics and AI-powered fraud and financial-crime protection | The acquired capability is described in Visa's product offering, but the filing gives no deal-level financial outcome. **Not proven from available data.** | Net cash was 4.1% of FY25 FCF ($887m / $21.577bn). Visa issued $3.924bn of senior notes in FY25, but the filing does not tie that issuance to this deal; it cannot be called debt-funded. | [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 2—Acquisitions, p.68; Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65] |
| Prisma Medios de Pago and Newpay | FY26 Q3 (nine months) | $1.500bn cash purchase consideration; $705m net acquisition cash in the nine-month cash-flow statement | Argentina issuer processing, real-time payments, ATM and bill-payment infrastructure; intended deployment of tokenisation, biometric authentication, risk tools and agentic-commerce capabilities | Closed in February 2026; purchase-price allocation remains provisional and no return, synergy, revenue, or payback outcome is disclosed. **Not proven from available data.** | Cash purchase price equals 9.9% of nine-month FY26 FCF ($1.500bn / $15.164bn); the filing does not quantify a divestiture, focus cost, or foregone option. | [Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Note 2—Acquisitions, p.11; Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.10] |

The three deals' disclosed purchase consideration totals $3.375bn. This is a repeated acquisition programme, but the available facts do not establish the serial-acquirer red flag: no transaction is disclosed as debt-funded, and the programme's deal-level outcomes are not yet reported. That absence is not proof of value creation; it is the reason M&A discipline scores only 12/20. [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 2—Acquisitions, p.68; Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Note 2—Acquisitions, p.11]

**Serial-acquirer + opportunity-cost test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4).** The company states strategic rationales for each deal, so RF-CAP-001 (large acquisition with vague rationale) is not triggered. But neither the annual filing nor the current 10-Q supplies the post-close return or synergy figures needed to show that the programme beat its opportunity cost. No RF-CAP-004 serial-acquirer cap is applied: the available disclosures do not establish the full pattern of repeated material, debt-funded acquisitions that the trigger requires. Future deal reporting that shows weak integration, further debt used for acquisitions, or a materially larger deal pattern would change this conclusion.

## 3. Buyback Scorecard

| Year | Amount Spent | Currency | Shares Bought | Implied Avg Price | Share-Count Change | Offset SBC Dilution? | Price-Discipline Evidence | Judgment | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|---|
| FY23 | 12,182 | USD m | 55m | $222.27 | FY23 comparative as-converted ending count is not in the FY25 table | Not determinable from the supplied point-to-point share-count table | Open-market shares retired; no intrinsic-value hurdle disclosed | Amber — net-retirement mechanics are clear, but value paid is not assessable in this module | [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 15—Stockholders' Equity, pp.88–90] |
| FY24 | 16,958 | USD m | 64m | $266.24 | FY24 total as-converted shares: 1.983bn; FY23 comparable not in the table | Not determinable from the supplied point-to-point share-count table | Open-market shares retired; average price rose 19.8% from FY23 | Amber — same limitation | [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 15—Stockholders' Equity, pp.88–90] |
| FY25 | 18,185 | USD m | 54m | $335.44 | 1.983bn to 1.930bn as-converted shares (−53m, −2.7%); Class A shares fell 1.733bn to 1.691bn | No — the ending as-converted count fell despite 5m shares issued under equity plans | All repurchased shares were retired; average price rose another 26.0%, and no valuation hurdle is disclosed | Amber — per-share count improved, while intrinsic-value discipline remains not assessable | [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 15—Stockholders' Equity, pp.88–90] |
| FY26 Q3 (nine months) | 16,537 | USD m | 50m | $328.29 | 1.930bn to 1.880bn as-converted shares (−50m, −2.6%); class conversions/exchange also changed the count | Not determinable: the B-share exchange and conversion-rate changes mean the reduction cannot be assigned solely to buybacks | Open-market shares retired; average price is below FY25's $335.44 but no intrinsic-value hurdle is disclosed | Amber — net count fell, but the causal attribution and value paid are not fully observable | [Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Note 11—Stockholders' Equity, pp.21–22] |

FY23–FY25 open-market repurchases totalled 173m shares and $47.325bn of total cost. The uses-of-capital table shows $47.130bn because it uses the cash-flow statement; the $195m difference is settlement timing and other cash-flow-versus-total-cost presentation differences. **Intrinsic-value discipline is not assessable from this module.** The observable record is a net share-count reduction, not merely an offset to equity-plan issuance, but the average repurchase price rose from $222.27 to $335.44 over FY23–FY25. [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 15—Stockholders' Equity and Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, pp.65, 88–90]

## 4. Dividends & Organic Reinvestment

| Metric | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Payout ratio | 22.1% of FY23–FY25 GAAP net income ($12.602bn dividends / $57.074bn net income) | [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Operations and Cash Flows, pp.58, 65] |
| Dividend coverage (FCF) | 4.76x for FY23–FY25 ($59.966bn FCF / $12.602bn dividends); annual coverage was 5.25x, 4.43x and 4.66x | [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.65] |
| Documented dividend policy exists? Followed for ≥4 years? (A10-01) | The company says it expects to continue quarterly cash dividends, subject to Board discretion and cash needs. Dividends rose from $3.751bn to $4.217bn to $4.634bn over the three available audited years, but no payout target and no four-year history are in the supplied filing. | [FY25 Form 10-K, Item 5—Dividends; Note 15—Stockholders' Equity, pp.35, 90] |
| Capex versus depreciation and amortisation | Capex was $1.059bn, $1.257bn and $1.482bn against depreciation and amortisation of $0.943bn, $1.034bn and $1.220bn: 1.12x, 1.22x and 1.21x. | [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows and Operations, pp.58, 65] |
| Incremental ROIC (Δ NOPAT / Δ invested capital) | **Not assessable on a reported basis.** Visa does not disclose a consistent invested-capital base or organic-only capital allocation, so a ΔNOPAT / Δ invested-capital result would depend on an arbitrary allocation of acquisitions, goodwill, settlement balances and client incentives. CIQ's reported return-on-capital series rose 23.1% (FY23) → 24.8% (FY24) → 26.9% (FY25), which supports a high company-wide return read but is not an incremental-ROIC calculation. | [CIQ Financials→Ratios, FY23–FY25, Return on Capital — vendor export; `ciq_facts.json`, `multi_year_trajectory`, data as of 2026-09-23] |

## 4A. Distribution Conduct (A10-05)

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Mechanism rationale stated (dividend vs buyback, who benefits)? | Visa states that the Board authorised a quarterly dividend and a multi-year repurchase programme. It says repurchases will be at prices it deems appropriate given market conditions and financial performance, but it does not explain a target split between dividends and buybacks or compare the two mechanisms on all-shareholder economics. **Amber.** | [FY25 Form 10-K, Liquidity and Capital Resources—Uses of Liquidity, pp.49–50] |
| Promoter participation in any buyback (tender/open-market) | Not Applicable — Visa has no promoter group. The disclosed programme is open-market repurchases, not a promoter tender; no director, named executive officer, or the group owned more than 1% of Class A shares at December 1, 2025. | [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 15—Stockholders' Equity, pp.88–90; FY26 DEF 14A, Beneficial Ownership of Equity Securities, p.110] |
| Buyback timing vs promoter pledge / stake changes | Not Applicable — no promoter group. The proxy says employees and directors are prohibited from pledging Visa stock; it does not itself provide a buyback-timing analysis against each insider transaction. No pledge-linked tender is disclosed in the reviewed documents. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Stock Ownership Guidelines, p.44; Hedging and Pledging, p.53] |

## 5. Capital-Allocation Read

**Verdict: value-creative / disciplined.** FY23–FY25 returned $59.732bn through cash buybacks and dividends, while FCF covered the dividends 4.76 times and the as-converted share count fell 2.7% in FY25; those are direct per-share positives. The best use was the combination of a low-payout, FCF-covered dividend and open-market repurchases that reduced the share count, although the filings do not establish that repurchases were below intrinsic value. The weakest use is M&A: $3.375bn of disclosed consideration since FY24 has clear strategic rationales but no disclosed deal-level return, synergy, or payback result. The single biggest capital-allocation signal is the current FY26 funding tension: identified nine-month cash uses were 162.5% of CFO and strict net debt rose $3.492bn, so a repeat of this pace needs to be judged against future cash generation and litigation cash needs rather than extrapolated from the FY23–FY25 record. [FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows and Note 15, pp.65, 88–90; Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows and Balance Sheets, pp.4, 10]

- [x] Uses-of-capital amounts and CFO percentages are shown, including the itemised total build.
- [x] Each material acquisition has price, purpose, disclosed outcome status and an opportunity-cost read; no M&A return is assumed.
- [x] Buyback average prices, retirement mechanics and the share-count qualifier are shown; intrinsic-value discipline is marked not assessable.
- [x] Dividend coverage is calculated from FCF; incremental ROIC is marked not assessable rather than constructed from an arbitrary capital base.
- [x] A10-01 and A10-05 appear in the Universal Findings Table.
- [x] Every Amber row has a follow-up; no Red capital-allocation trigger is supported by the available evidence.
- [x] No multi-year-cap applies: FY23–FY25 provides three audited annual periods.

## management-governance / 03_incentives-and-compensation.md

_Source: `03_incentives-and-compensation.md`_

# Incentives & Compensation — V

## 1. Compensation Structure

| Executive | Base | Annual Bonus | Long-Term Incentive | Cash vs Equity Mix | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| CEO — Ryan McInerney | $1.506m salary | $5.250m cash incentive (140% of $3.750m target; target = 250% of salary) | $18.723m stock awards + $5.688m options = $24.411m grant-date value | 22.7% cash / 77.3% equity | [FY26 DEF 14A, Summary Compensation Table, p.71; Annual Incentive Plan, p.61] |
| CFO — Chris Suh | $0.903m salary | $2.205m cash incentive (140% of $1.575m target; target = 175% of salary) | $5.849m stock awards + $2.250m options = $8.099m grant-date value | 28.0% cash / 72.0% equity | [FY26 DEF 14A, Summary Compensation Table, p.71; Annual Incentive Plan, p.61] |

Currency is USD. The period is Visa's FY2025, ended September 30, 2025. Cash/equity percentages use each executive's Summary Compensation Table total; stock and option amounts are grant-date accounting values, not cash realized. The CEO's target annual compensation was 95% variable and at risk; the other named executive officers averaged 91%. [FY26 DEF 14A, Executive Compensation—Performance-Based Compensation for FY2025, p.52]

## 2. Performance Metrics

| Plan | Metric | Weight | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| Annual bonus | Net Revenue Growth—VIP adjusted: 11.2% actual vs 9.8% goal | Not disclosed | Size-based | [FY26 DEF 14A, Annual Incentive Plan—Selected Corporate Performance Goals, pp.58–59] |
| Annual bonus | Net Income Growth—VIP adjusted: 11.5% actual vs 8.2% goal | Not disclosed | Size-based; absolute profit growth, not per-share profit | [FY26 DEF 14A, Annual Incentive Plan—Selected Corporate Performance Goals, pp.58–59] |
| Annual bonus | EPS Growth—VIP adjusted: 15.1% actual vs 12.2% goal | Not disclosed | Per-share / returns-based: earnings per share (EPS) measures profit for each share | [FY26 DEF 14A, Annual Incentive Plan—Selected Corporate Performance Goals, pp.58–59] |
| Annual bonus | Business drivers: Net Promoter Score; payment transactions, payments volume, cross-border volume, acceptance locations, token penetration and brand preference | Not disclosed | Mixed, but transaction, volume and acceptance-location growth are size/activity measures | [FY26 DEF 14A, Annual Incentive Plan—Selected Corporate Performance Goals, pp.58–60] |
| Annual bonus | Foundational and Company Objectives; some operating goals/results are withheld | Not disclosed | Not fully classifiable from available disclosure | [FY26 DEF 14A, Annual Incentive Plan—Scorecard Design and Goal Disclosure, pp.58–60] |
| Long-term incentive | Performance shares: average EPS—PS adjusted result against annual EPS goals over three years | 50% of target LTI value is performance shares; EPS is the primary payout metric | Per-share / returns-based | [FY26 DEF 14A, Long-Term Incentive Compensation—Performance Shares, pp.62–64] |
| Long-term incentive | Relative three-year total shareholder return (TSR) versus the S&P 500 | Modifier: 75%–125% of the EPS-derived share result | Returns-based; TSR measures share-price change plus reinvested dividends | [FY26 DEF 14A, Long-Term Incentive Compensation—Relative TSR Modifier, pp.63–64] |
| Long-term incentive | Stock options and restricted stock units (RSUs), each with three-year vesting | 25% options / 25% RSUs / 50% performance shares | Indirectly returns-linked through Visa's share price, but not performance-metric awards | [FY26 DEF 14A, Long-Term Incentive Compensation, pp.62–63] |

The 2025 annual plan paid 140% of target. The listed financial goals were exceeded, but payment-transactions growth was 9.3% against a 10.4% goal. That miss does not by itself contradict the payout because the Compensation Committee uses an unweighted, holistic scorecard; however, the lack of individual metric weights means the contribution of each goal to 140% cannot be assessed. [FY26 DEF 14A, Annual Incentive Plan—Scorecard Design and Selected Corporate Performance Goals, pp.58–61]

For the completed 2023 performance-share cycle, average EPS achievement was 136.7% of target and the 69th-percentile relative-TSR rank produced a 119% modifier, or 162.7% of target shares. This shows the long-term formula operated as disclosed, but does not cure the annual-plan weighting gap. [FY26 DEF 14A, Long-Term Incentive Compensation—Determination of Shares Earned, pp.65–66]

## 3. Pay vs Performance

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| CEO total pay (latest) | $31.561m in FY2025, up $5.561m (21.4%) from $25.999m in FY2024. The FY2025 total included $24.411m of grant-date equity value and $5.250m of annual incentive pay. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Summary Compensation Table, p.71] |
| CFO total pay (latest) | $11.247m in FY2025, up $2.470m (28.1%) from $8.778m in FY2024. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Summary Compensation Table, p.71] |
| Pay vs peers (if available) | Visa used a 21-company peer group and an independent consultant, but the proxy gives neither a CEO/CFO peer median nor Visa's percentile. A quantitative pay-versus-peer verdict is not assessable. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Compensation Peer Group, pp.55–56; Use of Market Data, p.57] |
| Pay-for-performance alignment | The 140% annual payout followed financial metrics above target, while performance shares use three-year EPS plus relative TSR. The annual scorecard still contains size/activity measures and has no metric weights. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Annual Incentive Plan, pp.58–61; Long-Term Incentive Compensation, pp.62–66] |
| Say-on-pay support (%) | Approximately 92% of votes cast supported FY2024 NEO pay at the 2025 annual meeting; this is above the 80% significant-dissent line. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Say-on-Pay, p.53] |

The Summary Compensation Table's grant-date treatment means the 21.4% CEO-pay increase is not a measure of cash realized. It is still a useful magnitude signal: it exceeds the 15.1% VIP-adjusted EPS-growth result that was one of the annual goals, while performance shares were also rewarded through the separate three-year formula. [FY26 DEF 14A, Summary Compensation Table, p.71; Annual Incentive Plan—Selected Corporate Performance Goals, pp.58–61]

## 3A. Statutory & Code Pay Gates (A6-01, A6-02, A6-05, A6-06, A6-09)

| Test | Raw value | Band applied | Verdict | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Aggregate managerial pay vs the statutory cap (India: Sec 197 — 11% aggregate; ≤5% single MD/WTD, ≤10% all executives, NEDs ≤1%/3%; loss years per Schedule V) | U.S. issuer; no Sec. 197-style statutory cap applies. CEO FY2025 total pay was 0.157% of $20.058bn GAAP net income. | Not applicable: U.S. law rather than the Indian statutory cap | Not Applicable | [FY26 DEF 14A, Summary Compensation Table, p.71; FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Operations, p.60] |
| Promoter-executive pay vs the extra gates (India: LODR Reg 17(6)(e) — ₹5cr or 2.5% of net profit individually, 5% aggregate) | No promoter group is identified. Visa's peer review covers 21 companies but does not disclose a CEO/CFO pay median or percentile. | Indian promoter gates do not apply; peer comparison cannot be calculated | Insufficient Data | [FY26 DEF 14A, Beneficial Ownership, pp.110–111; Compensation Peer Group, pp.55–56] |
| MD/CEO pay growth vs 3-year PAT CAGR | CEO total pay: +21.4% FY2025/FY2024. Three-year PAT CAGR is not used because total pay includes volatile grant-date equity values; FY2025 GAAP net income is $20.058bn. | No disclosed three-year matched pay/performance series suitable for this test | Insufficient Data | [FY26 DEF 14A, Summary Compensation Table, p.71; FY25 Form 10-K, Consolidated Statements of Operations, p.60] |
| CEO-to-median ratio + trend of the MEDIAN (rising exec pay on a falling median is the flag) | 204:1; CEO $31.561m, median employee $154,909. Visa reused the employee identified in 2023; prior-year median compensation is not supplied. | Amber: ratio is above the 200× watch line, but falling median pay or lack of justification is not established | Amber | [FY26 DEF 14A, CEO Pay Ratio, p.85] |
| Severance / change-of-control terms (double-trigger only; ≤2 years' pay) | Cash severance = 2× base salary plus target annual incentive; health benefits for two years. Change-of-control benefits require a qualifying termination. | Green for double trigger and two years of target cash pay | Green | [FY26 DEF 14A, Executive Severance Plan and Equity Incentive Awards, pp.81–82] |
| Clawback / malus policy (and: was any trigger acted on?) | Dodd-Frank/NYSE-compliant clawback; Board can also recover time-based RSUs/options after a fraud, intentional-misconduct or gross-negligence restatement. No qualifying FY2025 restatement is disclosed, so a recovery event is not applicable. | Green: restatement and misconduct coverage with a three-year mandatory rule | Green | [FY26 DEF 14A, Other Compensation Practices—Recoupment Policies, pp.53, 69; FY25 Form 10-K, cover page] |
| Problematic practices (repricing without a vote, gross-ups, single-trigger vesting, guaranteed multi-year bonuses) | No tax gross-ups; option/SAR repricing requires prior shareholder approval; no single-trigger change-of-control payment; NEO employment is at will. | Green: none of the specified problematic practices disclosed | Green | [FY26 DEF 14A, Highlights of Executive Compensation Program, pp.52–53] |
| Dissent response (any ≥20% vote against pay met with a disclosed response ≤6 months?) | 92% support; 8% against on the FY2024 pay proposal. | Green: below the 20% dissent trigger | Green | [FY26 DEF 14A, Say-on-Pay, p.53] |

Median pay's prior-year change is not disclosed. | Cannot test whether median pay fell while executive pay rose | Insufficient Data | [FY26 DEF 14A, CEO Pay Ratio, p.85] |
| ESOP pool breadth (broad-based vs top-concentrated); promoters excluded? | FY2025 EIP gross grants: 0.644m options + 2.788m RSUs + 0.476m maximum performance shares = 3.909m, or about 0.23% of 1.688bn Class A shares outstanding at October 30, 2025. The plan permits employee and NED awards, but recipient distribution and remaining-plan overhang are not disclosed. | Annual EIP grant rate is below the 2% Green band, but breadth and total overhang cannot be tested | Amber | [FY25 Form 10-K, Note 17—Share-based Compensation, pp.93–95; cover page—shares outstanding] |

## 4. Incentive Alignment Read

**Mixed.** Visa's largest long-term performance component is a 50% performance-share award tied to three-year EPS and relative TSR, while 95% of the CEO's target annual compensation is variable. But the 140% annual cash payout also rests on revenue, net-income, transaction, volume and other operating goals, and Visa does not disclose the weights or every goal; that prevents a conclusion that management is paid chiefly for per-share value. The single biggest signal is the contrast between a well-specified EPS-plus-TSR long-term formula and an unweighted annual scorecard that includes size-based measures. [FY26 DEF 14A, Executive Compensation—Performance-Based Compensation for FY2025, p.52; Annual Incentive Plan, pp.58–61; Long-Term Incentive Compensation, pp.62–66]

## management-governance / 04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md

_Source: `04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`_

# Ownership & Insider Behavior — V

**Scope and data note.** Visa Inc. is a U.S. GAAP, USD-reporting NYSE issuer. This report uses the FY25 proxy for legal beneficial ownership and the frozen Capital IQ (CIQ) ownership workbooks for later transaction and holder snapshots. The two are not interchangeable: the proxy includes shares obtainable within 60 days, while the vendor export reports a later ownership/trading view. The source-bound facts sidecar is the authoritative read of the CIQ workbook summaries used below.

## 1. Ownership

| Holder | % Held | Share Class | Bought or Granted? | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| CEO — Ryan McInerney (no founder-controller) | 0.049% beneficial: 823,086 shares = 274,569 held + 548,517 obtainable within 60 days | Class A | Original acquisition cost and grant history are not proven from the available table; the 548,517 option-eligible shares are not open-market purchases | [FY25 DEF 14A, Stock Ownership Information, p.110] |
| Other directors and executive officers, aggregate | 0.076% beneficial: 1,280,243 shares = 450,948 held + 829,295 obtainable within 60 days | Class A | Not proven from available data; the proxy identifies exercisable shares but not a bought-versus-awarded history | [FY25 DEF 14A, Stock Ownership Information, p.110] |
| All directors and executive officers (17 persons) | **0.125% beneficial**: 2,103,329 / 1,685,772,208 Class A shares; direct holdings are 725,517 / 1,685,772,208 = **0.043%**, with 1,377,812 shares obtainable within 60 days | Class A | A material part of the proxy total is option-eligible. The later CIQ snapshot reports 846,178 shares for its broader “Individuals/Insiders” category (0.05%); it is a different date and scope, so it is not a direct replacement for the proxy figure | [FY25 DEF 14A, Stock Ownership Information, p.110; Capital IQ Public Ownership Summary, latest export — vendor basis] |
| The Vanguard Group | 8.18% (141,408,295 shares) at the proxy record date | Class A | Investment-manager beneficial ownership; acquisition method not disclosed in the proxy | [FY25 DEF 14A, Principal Shareholders, p.111] |
| BlackRock Inc. | 7.32% (126,566,875 shares) at the proxy record date | Class A | Investment-manager beneficial ownership; acquisition method not disclosed in the proxy | [FY25 DEF 14A, Principal Shareholders, p.111] |
| Promoter / controlling bloc | No founder, promoter, government, parent-company, or other controlling bloc identified. The two disclosed 5%+ holders were Vanguard and BlackRock, each below 10%. | N/A | Not applicable | [FY25 DEF 14A, Principal Shareholders, pp.110–111] |

Total insider ownership is **0.125% beneficial** on the proxy basis. It is not meaningful owner-operator skin in the game: only 0.043% was already held, while 0.082% (1,377,812 shares) was exercisable within 60 days. No open-market insider purchase appears in the later 12-month CIQ record. This does not establish that every held share was granted; it establishes that cash purchases are not proven from the available record. [FY25 DEF 14A, Stock Ownership Information, p.110; data/V/ciq_facts.json, `insider_open_market`]

The later CIQ holder snapshot lists BlackRock at 137,669,061 shares (7.50%), Vanguard Capital Management at 115,685,867 (6.30%), State Street at 82,872,209 (4.51%), Capital Research at 57,815,206 (3.15%), and Geode at 45,285,275 (2.47%), all as of 2026-06-30. The source-bound sidecar reports these five as 29% of CIQ’s tracked-holder shares, not as a percentage of statutory shares outstanding; that scope qualifier is retained. [Capital IQ Public Ownership Summary, Top Holders, position date 2026-06-30 — vendor export; data/V/ciq_facts.json, `top_institutional_holders`]

## 2. Insider Transactions (last 12 months)

CIQ’s source-bound read records **net -291,641 shares across all transaction types** (22 acquisitions and 36 dispositions) through 2026-07-31. That total includes awards, option exercises, and related legs, so it is not a conviction measure. The cleaner open-market subset is **0 buys / 4 sales / net -32,180 shares**. [data/V/ciq_facts.json, `insider_net_activity` and `insider_open_market`]

| Insider | Buy / Sell | Amount | Date | Notes (10b5-1?) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Aggregate open-market activity | Sell | 32,180 net shares; 0 buys and 4 sales | 12 months through 2026-07-31 | The CIQ export does not attribute individual transactions to a Rule 10b5-1 plan | [data/V/ciq_facts.json, `insider_open_market`; Capital IQ Public Ownership→Insider Trading, per-event Form 4-derived rows — vendor export] |
| Kelly Mahon Tullier, Vice Chair / Corporate Secretary | Open-market sale | 19,991 shares; $7.29m at $364.81 | 2026-07-30 | Filed 2026-07-31. The sale followed the 2026-07-28 Q3 release; no timing flag follows from it | [Capital IQ Public Ownership→Insider Trading, 2026-07-30 Form 4-derived row — vendor export; Q3 FY26 earnings release, pp.1–2] |
| Chris Suh, CFO | Open-market sale | 10,639 shares; $3.46m at $324.81 | 2026-05-12 | Filed 2026-05-13. It followed the 2026-04-28 Q2 release; the available release reported 17% net-revenue growth and 36% GAAP EPS growth | [Capital IQ Public Ownership→Insider Trading, 2026-05-12 Form 4-derived row — vendor export; Q2 FY26 Financial Results Presentation, pp.1–2] |
| Lloyd A. Carney, independent director | Open-market sales | 650 shares at $309.62; 900 shares at $336.48 | 2026-03-11; 2025-11-04 | Two of the four open-market sales; plan status not disclosed in the vendor export | [Capital IQ Public Ownership→Insider Trading, 2026-03-11 and 2025-11-04 Form 4-derived rows — vendor export] |
| Ryan McInerney, CEO | Exercise-and-sale activity, not open-market purchase | 20,970 shares on 2026-06-29 and 10,490 on 2026-07-01, among other exercise-and-sale entries | 2026-06-29; 2026-07-01 | These rows pair option exercise with a sale and must not be counted as open-market buying. The Q3 10-Q discloses a new 10b5-1 plan adopted 2026-05-22, but does not link individual sales to that plan | [Capital IQ Public Ownership→Insider Trading, 2026-06-29 and 2026-07-01 Form 4-derived rows — vendor export; Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Item 5(c)—Trading Plans, p.42] |

Net insider activity: **net selling**, but the signal is limited. The only clean subset has four modest open-market sales and no buys; the larger all-types net sale is mainly unsuitable as a conviction measure because option exercises and retained-award legs are included. No RF-OWN or RF-MKT red flag is fired: the frozen pool contains no underlying Form 4 filing that ties a sale to adverse non-public information, and the cited vendor transaction data cannot alone trigger one.

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Directors, named executive officers, and employees covered by the policy | Not disclosed as a numeric balance | Not assessable | Visa’s insider-trading policy prohibits pledging Visa securities as loan collateral and margin accounts related to Visa securities | [FY25 DEF 14A, Hedging and Pledging Prohibition, p.69] |

**No pledging disclosed** in the reviewed proxy. The policy is a control, not a reported zero balance; therefore the actual pledge percentage is not assessable. No promoter group exists, no pledge increase or invocation is evidenced, and no pledge red flag is triggered. The upstream hard-disqualifier scan likewise found no disclosed trigger, while preserving the same qualification. [FY25 DEF 14A, Hedging and Pledging Prohibition, p.69; business-model/01_disqualifier-scan, 2026-09-23, §§1–4]

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | Not a promoter super-voting structure. Class A votes on ordinary shareholder matters. Class B and C common stock have no ordinary voting right, but vote in limited circumstances such as certain mergers, a core-payments exit, and specified charter amendments; their votes use the applicable conversion rate. | [FY25 Form 10-K, Risk Factors—Capital Structure, p.34; Note 15—Stockholders’ Equity, pp.90–91] |
| Controlled-company status | No controlling shareholder is identified in the proxy. Vanguard (8.18%) and BlackRock (7.32%) were the only disclosed 5%+ Class A holders; no director, named executive officer, or the 17-person group exceeded 1%. | [FY25 DEF 14A, Beneficial Ownership of Equity Securities, pp.110–111] |
| Shareholder bloc with board rights | No shareholder bloc with board-nomination or veto rights was identified. The B/C financial-institution holders have limited class protections on specified transactions, not a stated right to nominate directors. | [FY25 Form 10-K, Risk Factors—Capital Structure, p.34; Note 15—Stockholders’ Equity, pp.90–91] |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment *(Filter 6)* | Filter 6 does not apply on the available evidence: Visa is neither state-controlled nor a listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent, and no controlling conglomerate is identified. The legacy B/C classes nevertheless create a bounded conflict risk because current and former financial-institution clients may have different interests from Class A holders in significant transactions. | [FY25 Form 10-K, Risk Factors—Capital Structure, p.34] |

The control concern is limited, not absent. The B/C classes do not control routine elections or grant a shareholder group general super-votes, but the company itself says their financial-institution holders can have divergent interests on the specified major transactions. Separately, the charter’s 15% ownership cap (5% for competitors) can make a takeover harder; that is an entrenchment feature, not evidence that management controls the vote. [FY25 Form 10-K, Risk Factors—Capital Structure, p.34]

Q2 reported 17% net-revenue growth and 36% GAAP EPS growth; Q3 reported 14% and 10%, respectively. This is not a full insider-trading clearance: plan attribution and the underlying Form 4 filings are absent. | [Capital IQ Public Ownership→Insider Trading, 2026-05-12 and 2026-07-30 Form 4-derived rows — vendor export; Q2 FY26 Financial Results Presentation, pp.1–2; Q3 FY26 earnings release, pp.1–2] |

The export lists T. ## 4B. Ownership-Structure Hygiene (A3-08, A3-09, A3-10)

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Promoter-group classification (any reclassification to “public”? persons-acting-in-concert complete?) | Not Applicable. The U.S. issuer’s proxy describes directors, officers, principal Class A shareholders, and Section 16 reporting persons; it does not use the Indian promoter-group classification. No reclassification issue is identified. | [FY25 DEF 14A, Beneficial Ownership of Equity Securities and Delinquent Section 16(a) Reports, pp.110–111] |
| Beneficial-ownership chain (ultimate owners identifiable? trusts/offshore layers?) | The known 5%+ Class A holders and their voting/dispositive power are identified through filed Schedule 13G/A information. This establishes disclosure for the identified holders, not a complete beneficial-owner chain below reporting thresholds. | [FY25 DEF 14A, Principal Shareholders, p.111] |
| Takeover-code disclosure hygiene (India: SAST Reg 29 timeliness, creeping-acquisition limits, promoter ≤75%) | Not Applicable to Indian SAST. The U.S. proxy states that applicable Section 16(a) filers complied during 2025, but that statement does not substitute for a full takeover-code review. | [FY25 DEF 14A, Delinquent Section 16(a) Reports, p.111] |

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Visa has dispersed institutional ownership, not owner-operator alignment: the 17-person director/officer group beneficially held 0.125% of Class A at 2025-12-01, of which 1.378m of 2.103m shares were merely obtainable within 60 days. The clean 12-month open-market signal is net selling of 32,180 shares with no purchases; it is a weak negative signal, not evidence of misconduct. The biggest minority-holder issue is the legacy B/C class protection in specified major transactions, not a controller, pledge, or super-voting promoter. [FY25 DEF 14A, Stock Ownership Information, pp.110–111; data/V/ciq_facts.json, `insider_open_market`; FY25 Form 10-K, Risk Factors—Capital Structure, p.34]

# Board & Shareholder Rights — V

## 1. Board Composition

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Board size | 11 directors after the 27 January 2026 election; 10 are independent and Ryan McInerney is the CEO-director. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Director Nominees, p.11] |
| Independent directors (%) — vs the applicable floor (chair status decides ⅓ vs ½ in India) | 90.9% (10/11), above Visa’s 58% charter minimum and the NYSE majority-independent requirement. This is a US issuer; the India chair-dependent floor is not applicable. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Director Nominees, p.11; Director Independence, p.27] |
| Chair / CEO split? Chair non-executive, non-promoter? Designated (not rotating)? | Yes. John F. Lundgren has been the designated independent Board Chair since January 2024; McInerney is CEO. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Board Leadership Structure, pp.28–29] |
| Woman director / independent woman director where mandated | Four of 11 nominees are women (List, Morrison, Murphy and Rendle), all identified by the proxy as independent. No US gender-seat mandate applies. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Director Nominee Biographies, pp.16, 19–22; Board Composition, p.23] |
| Meeting count, longest gap, ID present in every quorum (A1-14) | Board met five times in FY25. The proxy says no director attended under 75% of applicable board-and-committee meetings; it does not state longest gap, meeting quorum, or an ID at every meeting. Its governance overview also says “100% attendance,” so the conservative reported per-director fact is only ≥75%. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Director Engagement, p.32; Key Governance Principles, p.7] |
| Audit / Comp (NRC) / Nominating committee independence | Audit & Risk (four members), Compensation (six) and Nominating & Corporate Governance (six) are all independent; Audit & Risk met seven times, Compensation six and Nominating four in FY25. | [FY26 DEF 14A, Committees of the Board, pp.29–31; Director Independence, p.27] |

## 3. Related-Party Transactions & Group Leakage

This is the minority-protection lens only. Agent 09 owns transaction-level A5 quantification and has not yet supplied an output. The proxy discloses an Audit & Risk Committee policy, but no separately quantified related-person transactions. “No amount disclosed” is not evidence that economic activity was zero. The business-model disqualifier scan reports no hard RPT trigger, but that scan does not make the missing transaction values measurable.

| RPT Type | Amount | % of Revenue | % of PAT | % of Net Worth | Arm's Length? | Source |

| Rent / other payments to promoter entities | Not Applicable — no promoter group disclosed | N/A | N/A | N/A | N/A | [FY26 DEF 14A, Beneficial Ownership, pp.110–111] |
| Outstanding receivables from related parties | Not separately disclosed | Not computable | Not computable | Not computable | Not proven from available data | [FY26 DEF 14A, Certain Relationships and Related Person Transactions, p.42] |
| Cash / deposits placed with a related financial entity | Not separately disclosed | Not computable | Not computable | Not computable | Not proven from available data | [FY26 DEF 14A, Certain Relationships and Related Person Transactions, p.42] |
| Borrowings drawn from a related financial entity (and the approved cap) | Not separately disclosed | Not computable | Not computable | Not computable | Not proven from available data | [FY26 DEF 14A, Certain Relationships and Related Person Transactions, p.42] |

| Ratio quoted | Numerator + its basis | Denominator + its basis | Matched-basis version | Basis mismatch? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Any RPT / revenue, PAT or net worth ratio | No transaction amount disclosed; no stated period basis | No ratio was published in the reviewed proxy | Not computable until 09 identifies a transaction amount and matching annual or period-end denominator | N/A — a ratio was not calculated |
| Any related-finance cash/deposit or borrowing concentration | No balance, approved cap or utilisation disclosed | No matching cash/debt balance scope disclosed | Not computable; no monitoring threshold can be set | N/A — neither side disclosed |

No related financial counterparty is identified in the reviewed disclosure. Accordingly, its equity interest, regulation, cap/utilisation and ID/auditor review are Not Applicable, not assumed benign. The stated approval process requires Audit & Risk Committee review before approval/ratification; it considers terms, relationship, dollar value, alternatives and comparability to an unrelated third party, and permits revision or termination of an unapproved transaction. [FY26 DEF 14A, Certain Relationships and Related Person Transactions, p.42]

## 5. Minority-Shareholder Protection Read

**Adequate.** Ten of 11 directors are independent, the chair and CEO roles are split, all directors face annual majority voting, and holders of 15% of Class A can call a special meeting. The board is not a blank cheque: three independent directors have more than 10 years’ tenure and two directors require disclosed board-limit waivers; the 07 integrity read is also coverage-limited, though it reports no Material-concerns or Disqualifying grade.

The biggest residual shareholder-rights constraint is that Class A holders cannot act by written consent and B/C holders retain special class protections over certain structural transactions. Those features, plus missing multi-year voting-result data and unquantified related-person transactions, keep this from a “strong protection” conclusion. No hard disqualifier was routed from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan`. [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan, §§1–3]

- [x] Independent-director percentage and chair/CEO split stated.

- [x] RPT section stays at the minority-protection boundary; no A5 verdict is asserted.

- [x] Takeover defenses, voting standards and written-consent restriction are inventoried.

## management-governance / 06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md

_Source: `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`_

# Candor & Disclosure Quality — V

## 1. Owning the Misses

No reported management-guidance miss is available to assess. The two completed comparisons in the supplied FY26 calls were met or exceeded: Q2 adjusted constant-dollar net revenue grew 15% and adjusted constant-dollar EPS 20% against Q1's qualitative guide; Q3 equivalent growth was 12% and 10% against Q2's guide. That is evidence of guidance delivery, not evidence of how management responds when it is wrong. [Visa Q1 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-01-29; Visa Q2 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-04-28; Visa Q2 and Q3 FY26 Financial Results Presentations, p.1]

| Miss (from 01) | How It Was Communicated | Owned or Obscured? | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| No completed management-guidance miss identified; Q2 and Q3 FY26 guidance was met or exceeded. FY26 full-year guidance remains open. | Not assessable: there is no observed miss in the supplied period on which to test ownership. Management provided its then-current outlook in the calls and presentations. | Insufficient Data — no adverse outcome to judge; do not infer candor from two favorable outcomes. | [01_management-and-track-record, 2026-09-23, §4; Visa Q1 and Q2 FY26 Earnings Calls, CFO prepared remarks, 2026-01-29 and 2026-04-28; Visa Q3 FY26 Financial Results Presentation, pp.20–21] |

## 2. Non-GAAP / Adjustment Aggressiveness

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Recurring "one-off" addbacks | Litigation provisions were excluded in each of FY23–FY25: $906m, $434m, and $2,533m. Acquired-intangible amortization and acquisition costs were also excluded in all three years. The FY25 filing describes litigation as variable and unrelated to underlying performance, but the three-year recurrence means adjusted earnings should not be treated as a clean run-rate. | [Visa FY25 Form 10-K, Item 7—Non-GAAP Financial Results, pp.41–44; earnings/06_earnings-quality, 2026-09-23, §§4–5] |
| GAAP-to-adjusted gap (magnitude / trend) | FY25 GAAP net income was $20,058m and non-GAAP net income $22,542m: a $2,484m, 12.4% uplift. GAAP operating expenses were $16,006m versus non-GAAP $12,906m: a $3,100m, 19.4% reduction. | [Visa FY25 Form 10-K, Item 7—Financial Overview / Non-GAAP Financial Results, pp.41–44; earnings/06_earnings-quality, 2026-09-23, §§4, 7] |
| Does adjusted framing flatter reality? | Yes, materially, because the bridge excludes costs that recur. The filing also provides the GAAP-to-non-GAAP reconciliation and warns that non-GAAP results are not substitutes for GAAP. That disclosure reduces, but does not remove, the risk that the adjusted series appears steadier than the reported series. | [Visa FY25 Form 10-K, Item 7—Non-GAAP Financial Results, pp.41–44; earnings/06_earnings-quality, 2026-09-23, §§4–5, 10] |

## 3. Disclosure Transparency

Visa discloses the core economic drivers of its single Payment Services segment: payments volume, processed transactions, revenue components, client incentives, and the relationship between those drivers and service/data-processing revenue. The same core KPI framework is present in FY23–FY25 reporting and the Q3 FY26 10-Q, with the filing noting that prior-period volume updates were not material; this is useful consistency but does not prove the five-year A7-03 Green standard from the supplied record. [Visa FY25 Form 10-K, Item 7—Payments Volume and Processed Transactions; Visa Q3 FY26 Form 10-Q, Item 2—Payments Volume and Processed Transactions]

The disclosure is less conservative in adjusted presentation: the reconciliations are detailed, but a recurring litigation exclusion makes non-GAAP profit more flattering than GAAP profit. No KPI was found to have been dropped or redefined immediately after deterioration in the available FY23–Q3 FY26 materials. [Visa FY25 Form 10-K, Item 7—Non-GAAP Financial Results, pp.41–44; earnings/06_earnings-quality, 2026-09-23, §§4–5]

## 4. Tone in Bad Times

The supplied Q1–Q3 FY26 calls are verbatim transcripts, but none contains a reported management-guidance miss or weak operating quarter. The closest adverse financial event is FY25's $2,533m litigation provision: Visa disclosed that GAAP operating expenses rose 30%, primarily because of litigation and personnel costs, and supplied the reconciliation; it then excluded the provision from non-GAAP results. [Visa FY25 Form 10-K, Item 7—Results of Operations / Non-GAAP Financial Results, pp.41–44]

Accordingly, bad-times tone and Q&A evasiveness are not assessable from this snapshot. The Q3 call's warning that unusually high June–July cross-border e-commerce should settle toward a more typical travel relationship is a bounded example of adverse qualification, but it is not a test of responding to a miss. [Visa Q3 FY26 Earnings Call, CFO prepared remarks and Q&A, 2026-07-28; earnings/04_guidance-consensus, 2026-09-23, §2]

## 5. Candor Read

**Mixed.** Visa is transparent about the numbers behind its non-GAAP measures and has not been shown to withdraw or redefine guidance quietly in the two completed FY26 comparisons. The strongest negative signal is the recurring litigation provision excluded every year from FY23 through FY25, culminating in $2,533m in FY25; it materially improves the adjusted series even though the reconciliation is visible. [Visa FY25 Form 10-K, Item 7—Non-GAAP Financial Results, pp.41–44]

There is no evidence in this pool of an actual guidance miss or weak operating period, so high-trust candor is not earned. The single biggest candor signal is the tension between transparent GAAP reconciliation and recurring costs labelled outside continuing performance. [01_management-and-track-record, 2026-09-23, §4; earnings/06_earnings-quality, 2026-09-23, §§4–5]

RF-DISC-002 (recurring "one-off" / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs)

- [x] RF-DISC-002 is emitted as a standalone literal line in Section 5.
- [x] A7-03 and A7-04 appear in the Universal Findings Table.
- [x] No banned phrases used.

Phase 1: D-1, D-2, D-3, D-4, D-5, D-8 and D-9 ran. D-1 located Visa's own history page and the Dee Hock memorial; D-2 reconciled the 1958 origin claim to the disclosed May-2007 incorporation/reorganization; D-3 found no former name for the listed parent, but did identify its predecessor network entities; D-4 ran against USPTO; D-5 found no corroborated co-address entity; and D-8/D-9 used the proxy, EDGAR and court-screen results. The U.S. has no comprehensive public national director registry, so directorship mapping remains coverage-limited rather than being called complete.

Phase 2 ran on Visa Inc. and the four reorganization lineage entities. It reached hop 1 and terminates at `budget_exhausted`: the current continuation permits a complete people sweep, but no paid or country-by-country registry pull for every foreign group subsidiary/founder. The four predecessor entities are `pending-12-reconciliation`, because agent 12 owns their company-level enforcement, courts and adverse-media sweep in this full governance pass.

A17-02 is Green: the 1958 claim is a disclosed business lineage, not an unexplained claim that the 2007 parent existed in 1958. A17-03 is provisional, not green: no phoenix pattern is shown by the available evidence, but agent 12 must complete the legal/enforcement read for the predecessor entities before the provisional entity grades can be closed.

**A1-05 verdict:** 24 people were registered: 11 Clean and 13 Minor concerns, no Material concerns and no Disqualifying grade. The 13 Minor grades are a litigation-status qualifier, not a finding of misconduct. The decisive fact is the current status of the Cai securities case and its related derivative action, not a regulatory or criminal record.

## 3B. Lineage & Phoenix Read (A17-02, A17-03)

Visa's own website says Bank of America launched BankAmericard in 1958, BankAmericard became Visa in 1976, and regional businesses were merged to form Visa Inc. in 2007. The SEC record agrees that Visa Inc. was incorporated in May 2007 and reorganized in October 2007, bringing Visa U.S.A., Visa International, Visa Canada and Inovant beneath it. [Visa, About Visa / “Pioneering payment technology”, retrieved 2026-09-23; Visa 10-K, Item 1, 2015]

The available record does not show that a predecessor failed, that assets were moved out of a failed company, or that a related party repurchased assets. The evidence instead describes an overt corporate reorganization. However, the legacy entities' court, regulator, insolvency, sanctions and adverse-media records are still pending agent 12's canonical sweep. Therefore the phoenix conclusion is “no pattern shown from evidence reviewed; pending legal reconciliation,” not a full clearance.
